Dario Canovi: "Roma, mezz'ora di follia. Inter, la sconfitta nel derby ha fatto bene"

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"La Roma? Un arbitraggio che ha rasentato lo scandaloso, dare quel rigore era già ridicolo, farlo ribattere ancora di più. Ho visto una Roma ingenua, che ha avuto mezz'ora di follia. Purtroppo abbiamo rivisto la partita contro il Rea Madrid, un primo tempo in cui la Roma poteva vincere con ampio punteggio e poi la sconfitta. Quando giochi in Champions, in certi campi, poi è sempre difficile". Così a TuttoMercatoWeb.com l'operatore di mercato Dario Canovi dopo la sconfitta della Roma contro il Manchester United da 0-2 a 3-2.

È la seconda volta che la Roma viene rimontata... "Direi più un problema di inesperienza. Poi mettici anche l'errore di Balerdi, che era stato il miglior difensore".

Cosa rimprovera ai giallorossi? "Poco. Nel primo tempo la Roma ha giocato molto bene. Ma la difesa poi, dopo il calcio di rigore, è andata nel pallone".

Può sorridere invece l'Inter. "Giocare in Olanda non è mai facile. Vincere 3-0 vuol dire aver recuperato pienamente dopo il derby. Non tutte le sconfitte vengono per nuocere".

Serie A: la Lazio di Gattuso continua a stupire. "Sta giocando bene, vince. Cosa dire? Ha il grande vantaggio di non avere le coppe. Quando non ci sono le coppe le squadre vanno meglio. Ad eccezione del Milan l'anno scorso".

Dove possono arrivare i biancocelesti? "Sicuramente in zona Europa. Non credo siano da Scudetto. Poi, si sa, nel calcio tutto è possibile".

OpenAI hires Uber President Jill Hazelbaker to lead communications, filling a critical role that was curiously vacant for 10 months

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

OpenAI has hired one of Uber’s highest ranking executives to oversee its global communications efforts, filling a key role that had been vacant for nearly a year as the AI company navigates a challenging political landscape and moves closer to an initial public offering.

Jill Hazelbaker is joining OpenAI as chief corporate affairs officer, the company announced on Tuesday. Hazelbaker, who had been president and chief corporate affairs officer at ride hailing giant Uber, will work alongside OpenAI’s leaders to “deepen public understanding of AI and strengthen trust in its mission,” the company said.

She will report to OpenAI co-founder and president Greg Brockman and starts in early December, OpenAI told Fortune.

“As AI becomes part of more people’s lives, how we communicate, engage, and earn public trust has never been more important,” Brockman said in the announcement, citing Hazelbaker’s experience and understanding of the “opportunities and responsibilities that come with building technology at global scale.”

Hazelbaker is a well respected communications professional in the technology industry. She has served as Uber’s President and Chief Corporate Affairs Officer for the past 11 years, and previously held senior leadership roles at Snap and Google. She is replacing Hannah Wong, who served as OpenAI’ chief communications officer (CCO) until January 2026. Since then, OpenAI’s Chris Lehane has been leading the team in the interim.

In the ten months since Wong’s departure, OpenAI has experienced a series of public controversies—most notably when its AI agents hacked the company Hugging Face during training in July. The 11-year old company has been criticized for not being sufficiently transparent and communicative about its operations, at a time when public concerns about AI’s potential harms to society, the labor market, and the environment are rising. Backlash to the massive AI data centers being built across the U.S. has emerged as one of the key issues in the midterm elections taking place on Tuesday.

In September, OpenAI told Fortune it was not rushing to fill the role, and was focused on getting the right candidate. Over the summer, it ramped up its search by hiring an executive recruiting firm.

OpenAI offered Hazelbaker the position in the spring, but she turned it down, as Fortune reported in September. Sources suspected her close relationship with Uber’s CEO Dara Khosrowshahi, desire to be on the East Coast for her family, and already high salary played a role in her declining the role.

It appears OpenAI has now made Hazelbaker an offer she cannot refuse.

Hazelbaker will remain on the East Coast, but will spend time in San Francisco, where OpenAI is headquartered, the company told Fortune.

“The work of building public trust has never been more consequential,” Hazelbaker said in the announcement, describing AI as the “most profound technological shift of our lifetime.”

NASA astronauts voted in the 2026 midterms from space. Here’s how.

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Election Day reached beyond Earth this year.

Three NASA astronauts aboard the International Space Station cast ballots in the 2026 midterm elections, with astronaut Anil Menon sharing a look at his orbital voting setup on Tuesday, Nov. 3. His polling place? The same small compartment where he sleeps.

In a video posted to X, Menon showed how he used his computer to complete an electronic ballot and send it to Harris County, Texas. Fellow astronauts Jessica Watkins and Luke Delaney also voted from the station.

"We filled out an electronic ballot and sent it securely to the election officials," Menon wrote.

So how does a vote get from orbit to an election office? It takes a few more stops than a trip to the mailbox.

According to NASA, astronauts can request an absentee ballot using a Federal Post Card Application. Once completed aboard the station, their electronic ballot travels through the agency’s Near Space Network, which also carries other communications between the station and Earth.

The ballot passes through NASA's Tracking and Data Relay Satellite System to a ground antenna in New Mexico, then heads to Mission Control in Houston before reaching the appropriate county clerk. NASA says the ballot is encrypted and accessible only to the astronaut and the clerk.

This isn't a new Election Day workaround. Texas lawmakers cleared the way for astronauts to vote from orbit in 1997. That year, NASA astronaut David Wolf became the first American to do so while aboard Russia’s Mir space station.

Nearly three decades later, the space station's sleeping quarters are still pulling double duty for democracy.

Assassin's Creed maker Ubisoft has reportedly made an offer for Arkane, Bloomberg reported on Tuesday. Ubisoft is one of at least three companies to submit a bid for Arkane. According to Bloomberg, Xbox is also considering selling Arkane to the studio's management.

Suno Says Jason Isbell Lawsuit Over Artist ‘Identities’ Must Be Dismissed: ‘Nothing Unlawful About That’

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The AI music company has filed its first major response to the novel lawsuit, which accuses it of violating musicians' likeness rights.

Suno is asking a judge to throw out a lawsuit claiming the AI music firm stole the artistic identities of Jason Isbell and other stars, arguing there’s “nothing unlawful” about seeking to replicate the “vibe” of a particular artist.

Isbell sued in August, accusing Suno of allowing users to “conjure up a beloved musician’s identity” by entering their name as a prompt in its AI song generator. It was a novel case centered on an artist’s likeness rights, unlike earlier litigation against AI labs over the use of copyrighted music to train models.

But in Suno’s first major response on Tuesday (Nov. 3), its lawyers say there is “nothing unlawful” about merely using an artist’s name to describe music, any more than it’s illegal for a human artist to say “let’s go for something here with more of a Jason Isbell vibe.”

“When people describe styles of music, they often use artists’ names as shorthand for the genre they perform in,” Suno’s lawyers write in a motion obtained and first reported by Billboard. “This case turns on the premise that the law somehow condemns that universal practice. In reality it does not; the First Amendment would not allow it to.”

Suno’s motion, which asks the judge to dismiss the case immediately, argues that Isbell’s lawsuit attacks “ubiquitous methods of communicating ideas” and would hurt the “creative and cultural ecosystem” if allowed to proceed.

An attorney for Isbell did not immediately return a request for comment on Tuesday.

Isbell and other artists sued in August, accusing Suno of violating artists’ likeness rights (also known as publicity rights) by encoding their entire musical personas as tokens in its AI model. The case was filed as a class action, seeking to potentially represent “millions” of other artists: “The platform is built to identify musicians by name and generate identity-evocative output on command. This is its product.”

Suno was already facing a slew of lawsuits from major music companies, who claim the company violated their federal copyrights by ingesting millions of songs to train their models. The new case was different, accusing Suno of breaching state laws in New York, California, Tennessee and elsewhere that protect a person’s name, image and likeness from commercial exploitation.

Suno says it doesn’t actually allow users to prompt its AI model with specific artist names. When users do enter artist names, the platform says it translates that request into “descriptive musical characteristics,” which can then be used to direct the model to generate a song. A prompt of “Duran Duran,” for example, might get redirected into one of “New wave, Synth-pop, Pop rock.”

Isbell’s lawsuit claims those safeguards are faulty and can be duped by merely using spaces, such as Taylor Swift with “t a y l o r s w i f t” or Bad Bunny with “b a d b u n n y.” Even when a name is translated into musical styles, Isbell says the model clearly still exploits an artist’s identity: “Suno built and trained a model to index musicians by name. It encoded musicians’ identities into its AI model to create its commercial product without consent.”

But in Tuesday’s response, Suno says those accusations are legally deeply flawed. Simply using the name of a real person as a “shorthand to convey information about the genre that they want to create in,” Suno’s attorneys argue, is not the kind of thing that has historically violated state-law likeness rights.

“The law does not prevent people from using names to find or describe information, nor does it prevent building a tool capable of recognizing those references and acting on the information they supply,” Suno’s lawyers write, arguing that a ruling to the contrary would violate the First Amendment’s freedom of speech.

Even if likeness rights worked that way, Suno says the case would still be doomed by federal “preemption,” a legal doctrine that bars state-level claims that interfere with existing federal laws. Allowing artists to sue to assert their state-level likeness rights in such a way, Suno says, would overlap too much with federal copyright law’s existing protections for creative works.

“Congress made the considered policy choice not to confer intellectual property protection on an artistic style, precisely to allow subsequent creators to build on the prior works of a genre,” Suno says. “[This lawsuit] would create a shadow state law IP regime … fundamentally conflicting with the federal regime that has been in place for more than two centuries.”

Stock Market Today, Nov. 3: Marvell Shares Edge Higher After Premarket Drop on $115 Billion Lumentum Deal

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Volatile trading marked Marvell's session today, Nov. 3, 2026, as investors weighed the all-stock deal and analyst downgrades.

Marvell Technology (MRVL +0.33%), a data infrastructure semiconductor provider, closed at $171.97, up 0.33%. Shares were volatile after a premarket sell-off tied to the company's $115-billion acquisition of Lumentum and several analyst downgrades.

Trading volume reached 37.6 million shares, coming in about 67% above its three-month average of 22.6 million shares. Marvell Technology IPO'd in 2000 and has grown 1,114% since going public.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%) rose 0.36% to 7,569, and the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +0.83%) gained 0.83% to 25,372. Among semiconductor equipment and integrated circuits for data center and networking infrastructure peers, Broadcom closed at $332.13, up 3.07%, while Arista Networks finished at $169.91, up 3.52%, as AI-infrastructure spending remained in focus.

On Tuesday, Nov. 3, Marvell shares gained the day after plunging about 13% on its announcement of a $115 billion all-stock acquisition of Lumentum, a provider of optical components critical to efficient data transmission, a desirable feature for artificial intelligence systems. The risk of share dilution from the deal combined with Toronto-Dominion Bank's TD Cowen issuing a Hold downgrade, contributed to Marvell's stock decline.

The fact that Marvell shares experienced an uptick today signals that Wall Street may have considered the stock oversold. After all, Marvell's move to own Lumentum is poised to turbocharge revenue growth.

Before the deal, Marvell had already raised its revenue outlook for its 2028 fiscal year (FY), ended Jan. 31, 2028, to $20 billion, and set a FY2031 target of $70 billion to $90 billion in sales. This represents massive growth from its FY2026 revenue of $8.2 billion.

Lumentum adds another fast-growing business to Marvell's offerings. Lumentum announced fiscal Q1 earnings on Nov. 2, reporting a spectacular 137% year-over-year increase in sales to $1.3 billion. It's no wonder Wall Street is reassessing the acquisition today.

iOS 27.3 beta: Everything new in Apples latest iOS beta release

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The new iOS 27.3 beta has just been released by Apple. So, what's new?

From a redesigned Health app to new vertical tabs in Safari, the latest iOS beta update for iPhone offers significantly more features than reports had previously expected.

Apple had previously redesigned the Health app before pulling that from a prior developer beta release. Now, it's back in the iOS 27.3 beta.

The new Health app has been redesigned to integrate Apple Intelligence into its core. There's also a more intuitive user interface, making certain categories easier to access. In addition, the redesigned Health app offers predictive insights into long-term health trends based on user activity and sleep patterns, including new features such as Health Age and movement assessments.

A whole new web browsing experience can now be found in Safari with Vertical Tabs.

Vertical Tabs in Safari provide a new design option where a user's open tabs are listed in a menu on the left-hand side of the screen instead of across the top of the app. Tabs open in Vertical Tabs can be displayed as an icon to save space or as the entire page title.

To try Vertical Tabs in iOS 27.3, users should go to the Settings app and tap Safari under Apps. The Vertical Tabs option will be located there.

The iOS 27.3 beta doesn't stop there with new Safari features. With the latest update, Safari now has Adaptive Dark Mode, which lets Apple's mobile browser automatically apply Dark Mode to pages that don't natively support the feature. Kurosawa Award Winner O Mipo Is ‘Making Up for Lost Time’ After Career Break to Raise Her Sons

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Co-recipient of the 2026 Kurosawa Akira Award together with Iranian filmmaker Mohammad Rasoulof, Korean-Japanese director O Mipo was in an understandably upbeat mood when she met with Variety just before the award ceremony on Tuesday at Tokyo’s Imperial Hotel, under the aegis of the 39th Tokyo International Film Festival.

The award honors filmmakers of substantial achievement who represent the future of cinema. O certainly fits that description: Since her industry start as a script supervisor for director Obayashi Nobuhiko, she has accumulated a long list of awards and accolades, beginning with a prize for her short “Eye” at the 2002 Short Shorts Film Festival in Tokyo. Related Stories Jamie Foxx Boards Indie Crime Thriller ‘East of the River’ as Producer (EXCLUSIVE) Joshua Jackson Teases ‘Sex, Drugs and Rock 'n' Roll’ in New HBO Max Series ‘How to Survive Without Me’: ‘I'm Getting Too Old For This S---’

In 2005 her first feature-length screenplay, “The Sakai’s Happiness,” won the Sundance/NHK International Filmmakers Award. Based on this script, she directed her debut feature of the same title.

In 2010, her drama “Here Comes the Bride, My Mom!” which she also scripted, won the grand prize for the Shindo Kaneto Awards, which are given to promising directors by a committee of Japanese producers. Her 2014 drama “The Light Shines Only There” was Japan’s submission for the Academy Award for best foreign language film.

After releasing the 2015 drama “Being Good,” O stepped back from features to raise her two sons, though she directed a segment of the 2022 international anthology “Tell It Like a Woman.” She returned to features in 2024 with the coming-of-age drama “Living in Two Worlds” and followed with the 2025 eco-themed children’s drama “How Dare You?”

“When I heard that they were going to give me the award my first reaction was just pure surprise – or rather, disbelief,” O says. “I couldn’t believe it. I wondered if they’d made a mistake. But I gratefully accepted it, of course. I hadn’t really made any big movies up to that point. I was just so happy that they found me out of all the (directors) in Japan.”

The Japanese film industry, she says, is “overwhelmingly different” since she first entered the industry as a crew member in 1999: “There are a lot more female staff members now, and directors too. That’s definitely the biggest difference.”

Also, what she describes as “the system for making movies” has changed. “Back in the day, the industry was like a pyramid, with directors at the top. That was my mindset, too, back when I was working as part of the crew.”

Now, she explains, “It’s become pretty common for everyone to go through what you might call ‘respect training.’ In terms of rights, everyone is more or less on the same footing – or rather, in the same position.”

But while production of Japanese films has been rising – nearly 700 are now released annually – O says she “can’t help but feel that the overseas market hasn’t really taken off yet.”

“I have a desire for my films to be recognized overseas,” she adds, “but the Japanese films I always see at film festivals are just… a certain type. So I’d like to see many more films get that chance.”

She would also like to follow in the footsteps of Kore-eda Hirokazu, Hamaguchi Ryusuke and other prominent Japanese directors who have made films abroad. “I’m thinking of giving it a shot myself,” she says. “I have a tremendous – call it ambition, or maybe just interest – to do that. I get asked that a lot: ‘What do you think of working overseas?’ I just come out and say, ‘I want to go to Hollywood someday.’”

In addition to making movies, O has long worked as a director of TV commercials and makes no apologies for it. “I honestly don’t think there are any directors in Japan right now who are making a living solely from directing movies,” she says. “They have to do other things – like streaming, TV dramas, commercials, and all sorts of other work – because you really can’t survive just from movies.”

“I’ve had a bit of a hiatus for about three years now because of raising my kids, but now that I’m back and have returned to filmmaking, the amount of time I have to devote to movies is enormous,” she adds. “When you’re making a movie, it takes up a huge chunk of your time. So even if I get offers for commercials, I have to turn them down. Gradually the number of offers dwindles and things have gotten a bit bleak since I’m relying solely on movies. I find that really ironic, though.”

In Hollywood, she notes, directors are “usually paid a proper fee right from the planning stage, though I suppose it depends on the scale of the project. It’s part of the contract, but in Japan, the reality is that you don’t get a single yen at the planning stage. And that’s been the reality for a long time. I’m going through that kind of experience right now, too.”

Turning to the subject of AI, she notes that it “has already become an inseparable part of our lives, and that includes filmmaking.” On whether machines will replace human filmmakers, she says, “I don’t think it will come to that.”

“I tell my sons that it’s important to have a sense of self,” she adds. “To always keep asking yourself, ‘What do I think about this?’ – no matter what the situation is. Of course, it’s fine to consult with AI – in fact, doing so will lead to richer results. But I tell them it’s better to constantly keep asking yourself, ‘What do I really want to do?’ And I try to be mindful of that myself.”

She takes inspiration from her mentor Obayashi, who started as a director of experimental films in the 1960s and went on to helm the cult sci-fi hit “House” and enjoy a long career mostly outside the Japanese industry mainstream. “He was always boldly taking on challenges,” she says. “It’s all about taking on challenges, right? It’s a constant battle but those challenges are like nourishment.”

“‘House’ came out the year I was born – 1977,” she says. “That makes it a movie from nearly 50 years ago, but when young people watch it today, they’re absolutely blown away. I think it’s a film that will never lose its appeal.”

That sort of longevity appeals to her – and not only for films: She notes that Obayashi died at age 82, Kurosawa at 88 and Shindo Kaneto – the scriptwriter and director whose eponymous prize she won – at 100.

“I’m 49, so I still have half a century left,” she says. “Another 50 years, so I still have a long way to go. There was a gap in my career when I had children. But I’m making up for lost time. And with this [Kurosawa] award I feel I’m getting back on track.”

Sonos is rolling out a big app update that includes new Presets and early access to its AI voice assistant. You can create a preset to play music at a specific volume in a certain room, which will appear directly on the homescreen so you can quickly start playback.

If you’re in the US, you can also sign up for a spot on the waitlist for the new Sonos 27voice assistant on November 6th, with access starting on November 12th.

As teased by Sonos previously, you’ll be able to use the assistant to do things like start music, move playback to another room, group and ungroup speakers, and set timers. But Sonos says it can also complete more complicated requests using natural language, such as “Play the album with a sardine tin on the cover,” or “Who was the singer at the superbowl? Play their latest album on Hallway.”

These updates are part of efforts to recover from a disastrous app redesign in 2024, which Sonos CEO Tom Conrad has promised to reverse. The company launched a revamped Sonos 27 app operating system update in September with the ability to connect AI agents to the app. Sonos plans to allow users to create up to 10 custom agents as well.

Other features coming to the Sonos app in this update include the ability to swipe on the “Now Playing” screen or mini player to skip tracks, and a way to rearrange your favorites. The Sonos app can also work with the iOS-only System Health feature, which can help you spot problems in your setup.

Data centers are turning to old jet engines as the wait for power grows

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Stuck in years-long lines for grid connections and new turbines, some AI data centers are making their own electricity with converted jet engines, the New York Times reported Tuesday.

The engines, pulled from retired aircraft and rebuilt to burn natural gas, aren’t anyone’s idea of a permanent power plant. But they’re another sign of how far power supply has fallen behind the AI buildout.

For companies stuck in those lines, the main draw of converted jet engines is how quickly the engines can be up and running. “It’s all about getting power as fast as possible,” Rystad Energy analyst Audun Martinsen told the Times. With jet-powered units, “they can have a data center running in 24 months,” he said.

Hooking up 100 megawatts to the grid in Northern Virginia, by comparison, takes about seven years, according to the International Energy Agency. Most power capacity is expected to come on by 2031, an executive at Mitsubishi Power, one of the world’s top makers of power generation equipment told Bloomberg last week.

In some cases, these delays are already costing some data center operators business. Last month, Microsoft pulled out as the anchor customer for Nscale’s data center in Loughton, England, after the local utility said it couldn’t deliver enough power until the early-to-mid 2030s.

Elon Musk’s xAI is among the companies already using the engines, having installed eight converted jet-engine turbines in Mississippi to help power its Colossus 2 data center in Memphis, the Times reported, citing state permits and satellite imagery. The units come from ProEnergy, which rebuilds a GE engine commonly found on Boeing jets.

In 2025, OpenAI followed Musk’s lead with gas turbines at its first Stargate site in Abilene, Texas. Crusoe, a Denver-based startup that builds AI data centers, is constructing that Abilene campus and ordered 13 of ProEnergy’s converted units in April.

In September, Crusoe walked away from a $1.25 billion deal to buy 29 natural gas turbines from Boom Supersonic, a startup developing a supersonic passenger jet that has also begun selling a version of that jet’s engine as a power plant for data centers. A Crusoe spokesperson told TechCrunch at the time that the company still plans to use turbines.

Currently the Trump administration is adding to the pressure by encouraging data centers to generate their own electricity.

Converted jet engines come with trade-offs, though: They’re less efficient and pollute more than turbines designed for conventional gas plants and it takes more of them to make the same amount of power, according to the Times. That may ultimately make converted jet engines a short-term play with some experts saying that efficiency and environmental concerns may grow in importance in a year or two.

The Origins of ‘Elf’: How Will Ferrell Came to Replace Chris Farley in the Christmas Classic

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In an exclusive excerpt from his new oral history, comedian Andy Richter — who has a small part in the movie — retraces the unlikely birth of the 2003 holiday hit.

The following is excerpted from Elf Unwrapped: Behind the Scenes of a Modern Christmas Classic – Andy Richter‘s Oral History of the Making of Elf the Film That Became a Holiday Classic, by Andy Richter (who had a small a role in the hit 2003 comedy) with Aaron Kino.

The story of Elf begins like most Christmas tales do — with a Jewish kid from Philadelphia. In 1993, an aspiring screenwriter named David Berenbaum moved from Philly to L.A. with dreams of making it big. He had no connections, no experience, and for the most part, no clue how to make it in “the biz.” But what he did have was an overactive imagination and a brain brimming with fanciful ideas. Related Stories Beyond the Book Book to Scream: How Literature Has Fueled 35 Years of Universal Orlando's Halloween Horror Nights Movies Before 'Star Trek,' Patrick Stewart Proved His Sci-Fi Chops in 'Dune'

David was a cockeyed dreamer, as many of us are when we land in Hollywood, but David just felt completely out of his element. Compared to the East Coast, L.A. is just . . . weird. No one seems to have a real job here, but they all talk like they’re showbiz big shots. People act like they’re your friend but then look past you mid conversation, just in case someone better walks by. David was lonely, especially when the holidays rolled around — which are even weirder in L.A. Suddenly, it was an unholy eight- five degrees around Christmastime! To cope, he rented a bunch of Christmas movies on VHS so he could watch them over and over. He binged classics like It’s a Wonderful Life, Miracle on 34th Street, Rudolph the Red-Nosed Reindeer — anything to feel a bit more Christmassy. Feeling inspired, he sat down to write his first Christmas film: a screwball romantic comedy called Christmas in New Jersey, which centered on two people having the worst days of their lives, who decide to completely start over as if they had never been born (very It’s a Wonderful Life coded).

Fortunately for him, that script actually got optioned — which is when a production company or studio pays you a little bit of money for the “option” of one day making the movie and then paying you a lot of money. Unfortunately, 99 percent of optioned projects never get made, and that was the case with Christmas in New Jersey. The good news, though, is that it opened up a ton of doors for David. Impressed by the quality of his writing, the film executives he met with kept asking him, “Okay, what’s next?” “Next??” David thought. “Damn, I better come up with something else!” Luckily, he was still in that Christmas frame of mind when he started working on his next big idea: the script that would eventually become Elf.

When David first started writing Elf in 1995, he was writing on spec. In Hollywood terms, “spec” means “speculative” — in other words, no one was paying him to write this script, so he was doing the work for free, hoping that he’d be able to sell it down the line. And though he loved a lot of the holiday movies he’d been bingeing, it was ultimately a 1964 Rankin Bass TV special that really inspired this particular project.

For some background: Rankin Bass was this quirky little production company that basically dominated Christmas TV programming for a couple of decades. If you’re a fan of Elf (and holiday movies in general), you’ll probably recognize their stuff right away. They specialized in stop-motion specials, the kind where the puppets move one frame at a time. Their most famous production, Rudolph the Red-Nosed Reindeer, has been on TV every December since LBJ was in the White House. But they also made Frosty the Snowman, Santa Claus Is Comin’ to Town, and The Year Without a Santa Claus — basically all those herky-jerky yet very magical holiday staples that feel like fever dreams nowadays but absolutely shaped everybody’s idea of what Christmas looks and sounds like.

Rankin Bass’s rendition of Rudolph the Red-Nosed Reindeer (adapted from a 1939 booklet published by the department store Montgomery Ward) follows the time-honored tale of Rudolph, a poor reindeer who gets mocked by all the other reindeer because of his glowing and very shiny red nose. After those mean bullies cast him out, he stumbles across a fellow misfit named Hermey, an elf who longs to be a dentist. Rejected by their peers at Santa’s workshop, they set out on an adventure, stumbling upon the Island of Misfit Toys and running into now-classic characters like the Abominable Snow Monster along the way. When they finally return home, Rudolph ends up saving Christmas by using his nose to guide Santa’s sleigh through a terrible snowstorm, proving that what made Rudolph different is exactly what made him special.

David knew what it felt like to be a misfit; after all, it wasn’t that long ago that he’d been working in a lingerie store, sweating in eighty-five-degree heat at Christmas. Essentially, he saw himself in Rudolph and wanted to create a character of his own that followed a similar sort of hero’s journey. Rudolph’s innocence also reminded David of Josh Baskin, Tom Hanks’s character in Big: a somewhat whimsical weirdo who doesn’t really fit in. Thanks to his wish being granted by a magical fortune-telling machine, Josh is quite literally bigger than all his peers, and this is also what sparked the idea of a giant (well, human-sized) elf named Buddy.

So, at this point, David has an idea with a great hook: a human who is raised as an elf in the North Pole. It’s a funny concept, it’s packed with potential for physical humor, and it also has this “fish out of water with a Christmas twist” angle that hasn’t really been done before. But as excited as he was in the moment, David knew he needed to flesh it out wayyyy more. He didn’t want it to just be a one-note gag. Plus, after watching all those Christmas movies, he realized the ones that really stuck around — the ones that reached the pantheon — had a little something extra.

In the original drafts of the script, when Buddy left the North Pole in search of his human family, both his mother and father were still alive. This presented a few problems — namely that it required a whole lot more explaining. It seems believable that a man might not know he had a kid, but what had Mom been doing for the past thirty years? Searching for her missing baby? Or just going on with her life, sipping margs by the pool and pretending nothing happened? Either way, it doesn’t exactly scream “jolly good fun.”

So David decided to follow in the footsteps of Cinderella and Snow White and pretty much every Disney movie and Grimm fairy tale ever — and killed off Buddy’s mom. Focusing the script around Buddy’s relationship with his dad made a lot of sense story-wise, but even more than that, it was a deeply personal choice for David. His dad had passed away when he was young, and this idea of Buddy searching for his father really resonated with him.

Ultimately, it was this emotional yet endearing through line that elevated Elf from a funny premise or silly sketch into a full-blown movie with the potential for real staying power. Before, the drafts felt a lot more jokey, but with that last piece in place, the script finally transformed into what he’d wanted it to be all along: a holiday comedy with a whole lot of heart.

DAVID BERENBAUM, SCREENWRITER: I just went back to what would be the most personal connection for me — my dad’s passing — so that’s why I made that choice. Once I found that, the writing became a whole lot easier and I had something that I thought was good. Plus, I thought, comedically, it’d be funny for Buddy to go, “I’m here, Dad!”

In 1996, after writing many (many, many, many) drafts of the script, David sold his screenplay for millions of dollars, and it was smooth sailing right into production.

Of course that’s not what happened. Instead, the film got stuck in “development hell,” the term for projects in Hollywood that languish with production companies and studios for years and never actually get the green light.

First, Elf was optioned by the Motion Picture Company of America (aka MPCA), a production company known for making critically acclaimed classics like the Academy Award–nominated Ulee’s Gold (starring Peter Fonda) and Beverly Hills Chihuahua 2 (starring George Lopez). It was while MPCA held the reins that the very first actor was attached (or in Hollywood speak, “verbally agreed”) to play Buddy: the one and only Chris Farley, fresh off Saturday Night Live. Once he was on board, David began refining the script with Chris in mind, and, at several points, was even scheduled to sit down with Chris himself so they could go over the new material. Alas, that meeting never materialized, and after a year, MPCA’s exclusive option period expired, relegating the what-if of Chris Farley starring in Elf to Hollywood legend.

Then, another production company optioned Elf, but after that endeavor didn’t work out either, the revised script earned David acceptance into the prestigious Disney Writing Program. This program brings in a couple of writers each year and has them rewrite whatever is in production at the time. It’s a trial by fire—Disney-style—but it’s also an incredible opportunity. David spent two years there, working on anything and everything they threw his way; in fact, one film he wrote there — The Haunted Mansion, starring Eddie Murphy—actually came out a few weeks after Elf. During this time, Disney also had the opportunity to make Elf, but alas, once again, for the innumerable and inscrutable reasons that most Hollywood projects die, it never came to fruition.

Luckily, the wait to bring Elf to life would soon be over, once a fairy godmother in the form of another SNL legend came along.

The people who eventually brought Elf to the big screen came from wildly different corners of Hollywood, from nobodies to legends, executives to improvisers, Comedy Central to Saturday Night Live. It was a jolly band of misfits, and the strange alchemy that united them — part design, part luck — would end up paying incredible dividends.

In 1999, a subversive sitcom called Strangers with Candy premiered on Comedy Central. Cocreated by a group of Chicago improv comedians (including Stephen Colbert and future Elf scene-stealer Amy Sedaris), the show was an offbeat, crass send-up of after-school specials — those preachy ’90s TV movies that aired right when kids got home from school and taught very obvious lessons about very serious issues. Strangers with Candy set out to offend audiences everywhere — and it did so brilliantly. There’s this truly unhinged episode in the third and final season in which Amy’s character discovers that she has Native American heritage and tries to learn more about her “roots.” I make a cameo in this episode, as does a tall, goofy up-and-coming SNL cast member named Will Ferrell. This was actually the first time Will and I worked on the same project. But more importantly (at least for the tale at hand), Strangers with Candy was produced by Comedy Central executive Kent Alterman, who would also go on to play a pivotal role in the Elf saga. So maybe the true reward of making weird television that doesn’t quite get its due is the friends you make along the way.

In 2000, David Berenbaum sat down with Jon Berg, a fresh-faced manager/producer who loved David’s Elf script — even if nobody wanted to make it, yet. Both Jon and David were nobodies at the time, but Jon was really impressed by David’s script and offered to become his manager. Before long, Jon and his producing partner Todd Komarnicki came on board as producers — essentially agreeing to hustle, unpaid, to try to get the movie made (and then get paid). Obviously, finding just the right Buddy would be crucial to getting this thing off the ground, and the two of them had a very specific person in mind for the role.

WILL FERRELL (BUDDY): The first time I heard about Elf was when I got a call from Julie Wixson Darmody in Jimmy Miller’s office. She was like, “Hey, there’s this script. The basic premise is a human raised at the North Pole who has to go to New York. It probably needs some work, but you should take a look.” I read it and I was like, “Oh yeah. I see what you mean. This could be something . . .”

The fallout from last month's departure of Xbox's newly appointed COO just got a little messier.

ICYMI, late last month, Helen Chiang, who had been with Microsoft for 21 years and was appointed COO in July as part of the big Xbox reset, abruptly left the company. Chiang has yet to provide an explanation for her departure, and the conspicuously brief memo in which Xbox CEO Asha Sharma announced Chiang's departure only deepened the mystery. This week, Chiang herself shot down reports that she was fired for violating policy and said she had "serious concerns" about Microsoft's handling of the situation, but notably didn't directly refute claims she was ousted.

Now, a prominent figure in Xbox's history is stepping up in defense of Chiang with a sharp rebuke of Microsoft leadership. In a response to Chiang on LinkedIn (captured via Twitter user Knoebel and independently verified), former Xbox VP Shannon Loftis, who was with Microsoft for 33 years before leaving in 2022, throws her full support behind Chiang amid her departure.

"Team Helen all the way," says Loftis. "The 'leak' itself (of false information, for what purpose -? Let's all speculate on who made THAT decision) shows the loss of moral character and 'clarity' in leadership at Xbox. A real leadership team would have relentlessly pursued the 'leaker' and taken appropriate action because 'confidential personnel matters' actually are bases for defamation lawsuits."

The "leaker" Loftis is referring to appears to be the source at Eurogamer who had claimed Chiang was fired for "policy violations." Chiang has vehemently denied the claim about why she was fired, but so far Xbox and Microsoft have provided no official statement beyond the published internal memo from Sharma, which provides virtually zero explanation for Chiang's departure.

"It's a clownshow up in there and I feel terrible for anyone that still has to deal with it," Loftis concludes.

Loftis is a self-employed "video game maker" today, according to her LinkedIn, but she originally joined Microsoft as an IT project manager way back in 1993, before hopping over to the games side of the business in 1995. She would climb all the way up to VP of Xbox Game Studios in 2022, a position she would hold for just a few months.

Fresh European Fridge Attacks Put Cold-Chain Security in Focus

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Beyond food storage, the same combination of Internet connectivity, remote access, and inadequate security practices can put temperature-controlled infrastructure across other critical sectors at risk.

A second wave of attacks on supermarket refrigeration systems across Europe this week has heightened concerns about the broader "cold chain's" vulnerability to similar attacks, given that supermarkets aren't the only businesses that rely on cold storage.

As in mid-October, an unknown threat actor appears to have gained access to refrigeration controllers at multiple European supermarkets and switched the systems to defrost mode, raising temperatures to 50 degrees Celsius (122 degrees Fahrenheit) and causing food spoilage.

Reporting by the BBC, Reuters, CNBC, and other outlets suggest the latest attacks were smaller in scope compared to the first wave in October that impacted more than 700 retail and grocery stores primarily in France. A smaller number of stores in Belgium, Germany, the Netherlands, Portugal, Poland and Spain were also caught up in the October attacks.

Related:Les supermarchés de l'UE paient d'un firmware de réfrigérateur non mis à jour

BBC reported the new attacks as impacting "dozens of supermarkets" in seven European countries including Belgium, Luxembourg, Estonia, Ireland, Sweden and Finland. Spokespersons from several supermarket chains like France's Carrefour, Belgium's Ahold Delhaize and Colruyt Group, and Sweden's Coop chains confirmed varying but relatively limited impacts from the new attacks.

In a Nov. 2 statement, Carrefour said a cyberattack targeting its refrigeration systems had impacted 15 out of 700 stores in Belgium. In an undated statement, US-based Copeland, which manufactures technologies for regulating commercial refrigeration systems, alluded to a cybersecurity incident affecting refrigeration systems in France, which it said related to a discontinued generation of supervisory controllers.

No individual or group has claimed responsibility for either the first or the second wave of attacks, and the motivation behind it remains a mystery. European authorities are currently investigating the attacks.

Researchers, including those at France's CERT, have linked the hacks so far to Internet-connected refrigeration controllers made by Denmark-based Danfoss, Italy-based Dixell, and Copeland. Organizations using these controllers often connect them to the Internet to enable remote maintenance and support, but in doing so also potentially expose them to unauthorized access and remote attack by threat actors.

Risks include unpatched vulnerabilities in these technologies that attackers can exploit, like three in Danfoss' AK-SM 800A controllers that researchers at Claroty disclosed this August and another two they reported the same month in Copeland controllers. Weak configurations and poor security hygiene are also other major problems.

Related:AI Takes Control of Robotic Arm in 16 Minutes Flat

"Misconfigurations and systemic hygiene failures represent the vast majority of the threat surface," says John Gallagher, vice president at operational technology (OT) and Internet of Things (IoT) security vendor Viakoo. "While unpatched vulnerabilities exist in older firmware, they are rendered dangerous only because basic architectural hygiene was skipped, especially [when] making them Internet-facing."

Common mistakes include widespread use of default passwords, vendor-shared service codes, or static credentials across hundreds of locations makes brute-forcing trivial, Gallagher adds.

The weaknesses are not confined to supermarket refrigeration systems. The same combination of Internet connectivity, remote access, and inadequate security practices can put temperature-controlled infrastructure across other critical sectors at risk, according to security experts.

Sean Tufts, field chief technology officer (CTO) at Claroty, points out that "pharmaceutical storage and distribution, food distribution centers, and industrial facilities all rely on connected operational technology to maintain precise environmental conditions."

Related:EU Grocers Pay a Steep Price for Unpatched Fridge Firmware

The level of exposure will vary depending on how these systems are deployed, connected, and secured. But many organizations that rely on remotely accessible refrigeration or temperature-control equipment face similar challenges around asset visibility, third-party access, and securing legacy systems, Tufts says. "The same types of weaknesses can exist across different industries, even if the equipment and operational requirements differ."

The potential consequences of a compromise, says Tufts, can vary by sector.

"A disruption to pharmaceutical storage could compromise temperature-sensitive medicines, while an incident affecting food distribution could lead to spoilage and supply chain disruptions," he notes. "Industrial environments may face additional operational consequences depending on the materials and processes involved."

The supermarket attacks are a reminder that refrigeration and temperature-control systems are part of essential operations and not just back-of-house equipment, Tufts adds. Organizations should identify these assets and know how they connect to the Internet and third parties.

"Where remote access is necessary, use a VPN, strong authentication, or a centralized secure-access tool with session tracking." Changing default passwords, keeping firmware current, and segmenting are also recommended security practices, he says.

Gallagher says that while it's unclear how the attacks on the commercial refrigeration systems played out, the most straightforward theory is the threat actor likely compiled a target list by scanning the public Internet for specific controller fingerprints, such as those tied to Danfoss, Dixell, and Copeland. They then likely deployed an automated script across that target list to exploit known web vulnerabilities or push default credentials, triggering the defrost mode simultaneously.

Gallagher says the same attacks that worked on commercial refrigeration systems can potentially work across almost any form of OT and IoT systems. That's because these systems often are managed outside of IT, lack automated controls to keep firmware and passwords updated, and have porous network borders. "The growth over the last three years in operational ransomware and in cyberattacks causing physical damage shows that these attacks are on the rise and not specific to any one kind of system."

Illinois-based Jai Vijayan is a veteran, award-winning technology journalist with more than 25 years of experience covering cybersecurity. His information security reporting has explored everything from ransomware, nation-state threats, and identity security to AI risk, critical infrastructure protection, software supply chain security, cloud security and emerging enterprise technologies.

Over the course of his career, Jai has written news stories, feature articles, survey reports, white papers, and e-books for enterprise and technology audiences. He has also moderated panel discussions and executive roundtables featuring CISOs, security researchers, and industry leaders.

Jai previously served as senior editor at Computerworld, where he covered information security and data-privacy issues. His work has also appeared in CSO Online, InformationWeek, The Christian Science Monitor Passcode, The Economic Times, and other publications.

His work has earned multiple industry honors, including a Joint ASBPE Excellence Award for Best Coverage of Government IT, and a Joint Jesse H. Neal Award for wireless LAN security coverage. Jai holds a Master’s degree in statistics from Bangalore University, and studied broadcasting and electronic communication at Marquette University in Milwaukee.

Stock Market Today, Nov. 3: Grab Holdings Jumps 9% on Q3 Revenue Beat and Raised 2026 Outlook

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On Nov. 3, 2026, shares of the Southeast Asian super-app climbed as profitability improved across all three core segments for the first time.

Grab Holdings (GRAB +9.09%), a Southeast Asian super-app for ride-hailing, delivery, and fintech services, closed at $3.12, up 9.09%. The stock rose after Grab reported Q3 revenue above estimates and raised its full-year 2026 outlook. Investors are watching whether the higher guidance and improved profitability extend the move. Trading volume reached 144.8M shares, coming in about 143% above its three-month average of 59.7M shares. Grab Holdings IPO'd in 2020 and has fallen 74% since going public.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%) rose 0.36% to 7,569, and the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +0.83%) gained 0.83% to 25,372. In internet services: ride-hailing, delivery, and digital financial services, Uber Technologies (UBER -0.17%) closed at $66.61, down 0.15%, while DoorDash (DASH -3.55%) closed at $183.32, down 3.55%, as delivery-platform peers digested growth and margin updates.

Judging from its excellent Q3 earnings, Grab remains an intriguing, under-the-radar growth stock in my eyes. Management announced:

In addition to these promising figures, management raised full-year guidance to 25% sales growth and a 59% increase in adjusted EBITDA.

Though management also announced that the company's proposed acquisition of foodpanda's Taiwanese business for $600 million was canceled -- hitting GRAB with a $60 million breakup fee -- it was an otherwise great quarter all around.

Home to a net cash balance of roughly $6 billion, this fee is barely noticeable to the company, and Grab will likely continue to rely on acquisitions for future growth as it builds out the depth of services offered by its superapp.

Trading with a forward EV/EBITDA of 10 (using management's adjusted EBITDA guidance), Grab's high-growth figures and improving profitability are very reasonably priced.

Transfer Online Partners with OnchainTA, a Digital Transfer Agent, to Expand Tokenized Securities Services

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The partnership establishes a framework for OnchainTA to provide tokenized securities capabilities to Transfer Online

FULLERTON, Calif., Nov. 3, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Akemona, Inc., a capital markets infrastructure provider for tokenized securities, today announced a partnership between its wholly owned subsidiary, Akemona TA Services, LLC, doing business as OnchainTA, and Transfer Online, Inc. (TOL). OnchainTA is an SEC-registered Digital Transfer Agent focused exclusively on tokenized securities, while TOL is a full-service, SEC-registered transfer agent. Under the signed agreement, OnchainTA will provide digital transfer agent services powered by Akemona's tokenization platform to TOL, enabling TOL to expand its services for tokenized securities.

Since 1999, TOL has provided transfer agent services to private and public companies, combining experienced human support with a technology-forward shareholder management platform, compliance-focused operations, and real-time reporting.

The relationship is designed to allow TOL to extend its issuer-facing transfer agent services to tokenized securities. TOL will remain the transfer agent of record and the primary point of contact for participating issuers, while OnchainTA will provide assigned tokenization and digital securities services through TOL-approved workflows.

"Transfer Online has always invested in its own technology, and that commitment keeps us at the forefront of the transfer agent industry," said Bryan Cochran, Director of Operations at Transfer Online. "For tokenization, we chose to partner with proven experts in the space to complement the platform we've built in-house. OnchainTA's digital securities capabilities let us offer issuers what's next in capital markets, while TOL remains their transfer agent of record and the team they already trust."

OnchainTA's Digital Transfer Agent services may include token setup, digital security recordkeeping, transfer and corporate action support, and reconciliation reporting. The scope of services for each issuer or offering will be mutually agreed by the parties.

"Rather than reinvent what specialists already do well, we brought in a leading tokenization partner and integrated their capabilities into our existing operations," added Cochran. "Every engagement runs through TOL-defined workflows, with full oversight and real-time visibility. Our team will support issuers from token setup through transfers, corporate actions, and reconciliation, and we plan to begin offering tokenization to Transfer Online clients early next year."

The agreement reflects a model developed for established transfer agents that want to add tokenized securities capabilities seamlessly alongside their existing operations. Services will be delivered through TOL-approved workflows and mutually agreed operating procedures.

"Transfer Online brings decades of transfer agent experience, a strong issuer-service model, and a practical commitment to technology," said Alex de Lorraine, Chief Executive Officer of Akemona. "Winning TOL's RFP and completing the partnership agreement are important validations of our approach. OnchainTA gives established transfer agents a way to add digital securities capabilities directly within their existing operating and compliance frameworks."

The relationship comes as OnchainTA prepares to introduce OnchainTA Release 2 shortly. Built on Akemona's tokenization infrastructure, the release will expand self-service capabilities across the Issuer Hub, Investor Portal, Admin Console, and related tokenization workflows. TOL personnel will receive training and support on the platform so they can obtain the records, reports, transaction information, and exception data needed to oversee each engagement and serve participating issuers.

OnchainTA Release 2 will include peer-to-peer Direct Transfer capabilities for compliant bilateral transfers of tokenized securities, subject to investor eligibility, identity verification, applicable transfer restrictions, and transfer agent controls. It will also include Cap Table Tokenization (CTT), which will enable verified issuer cap table positions to be converted into administered digital securities through a controlled process, and Digital Cap Table (DCT) capabilities designed to record, reconcile, and present digital security ownership information across complex ownership structures.

Akemona TA Services, LLC, doing business as OnchainTA, has operated as an SEC-registered Digital Transfer Agent for tokenized securities since 2021. OnchainTA combines registered transfer agent functions with Akemona's tokenization technology to support the administration of digital securities throughout their lifecycle.

"OnchainTA Release 2 brings together capabilities that issuers, transfer agents, and investors need as digital securities move into practical use," said Ravi Srivastava, Founder and Chief Product Officer of Akemona. "Peer-to-peer Direct Transfer, Cap Table Tokenization, and Digital Cap Table are being designed as connected parts of the same operating environment. Our work with Transfer Online gives us a strong framework for delivering those capabilities through an experienced, full-service transfer agent."

The companies expect to begin implementation planning and onboarding following completion of the applicable engagement documentation. Commercial terms were not disclosed.

About Transfer OnlineTransfer Online, Inc. is a full-service, SEC-registered transfer agent based in Portland, Oregon. The company provides stock transfer, registrar, equity administration, compliance, and related services to private and public companies through a secure, customizable platform. For more information, visit www.transferonline.com.

About OnchainTAAkemona TA Services, LLC, doing business as OnchainTA, is a wholly owned subsidiary of Akemona, Inc. and an SEC-registered Digital Transfer Agent focused exclusively on tokenized securities. Since 2021, OnchainTA has supported tokenization, digital security recordkeeping, transfers, corporate actions, distributions, redemptions, and related administration through Akemona's technology platform. For more information, visit www.onchainta.com.

About AkemonaSince 2018, Akemona, Inc. has provided regulatory-compliant capital markets infrastructure for tokenized securities. The company develops secure and scalable digital asset technology for capital markets. Its Tokenization Cloud and suite of applications, including CapMark AI Assistant, enable transfer agents, broker-dealers, asset managers, issuers, and other capital markets participants to build and operate digital asset workflows and applications.

Product Availability NoticeReferences to OnchainTA Release 2 describe planned capabilities and anticipated availability. Features, scope, and timing may change based on testing, regulatory requirements, implementation needs, and other considerations. This communication is for informational purposes only and is not investment advice or an offer to sell or a solicitation of an offer to buy any security.

BYD’s flagship luxury EV with 1,000+ km of range hits 4,000 sales in just 19 days

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

BYD wasted no time, with sales of its new flagship electric sedan reaching 4,000 in just 19 days since its launch.

With a record of more than 300,000 electric vehicles sold last month, BYD sold more EVs than the US did in the entire third quarter, combined.

A big part of the higher demand is thanks to BYD’s latest breakthrough technology, including its second-generation Blade Batteries and Flash Charging technology.

BYD began equipping vehicles with the new battery and charging tech in March, and over the past six months it has posted higher year-over-year sales growth. Through October, BYD sold 1,953,700 vehicles, up 6% compared to the same period in 2025.

On October 13, BYD launched the Great Han, or “Da Han” in China, the first fully electric D-segment flagship sedan in its Dynasty lineup.

Just 19 days after its launch, BYD said Great Han EV sales reached 4,000 units. At that rate, sales could reach 10,000 or more in its first full month.

With “class-beating high value,” BYD said, “it has firmly established itself in the mid-to-high-end sedan segment above 250,000 yuan,” which is about $37,500.

The Great Han is available in three variants, priced from 235,900 yuan, or about $35,000. Buyers can choose from single- or dual-motor powertrains. The standard rear-wheel-drive (RWD) Premium trim offers up to 1,008 km (626 miles) of CLTC range, while the all-wheel-drive (AWD) Flagship variant offers up to 880 km (546 miles).

All models use a 102.326 kWh BYD Blade 2.0 battery pack. The RWD models have a single 370 kW (496 hp) motor, while the AWD variant adds a 200 kW (268 hp) front motor for a combined 570 kW (764 hp).

Like most of its new vehicles, the Great Han has a roof-mounted LiDAR and BYD’s God’s Eye B, a more advanced version of its intelligent driving system.

The ADAS system offers safety and convenience features, including Navigate on Autopilot on highways and in cities, plus remote autonomous parking.

All variants also include premium features, such as BYD’s DiSus-A dual-chamber air suspension system, rear-wheel steering, and zero-gravity front seats.

Measuring 5,256 mm in length, 1,999 mm in width, and 1,510 mm in height, with a 3,130 mm wheelbase, the Great Han is slightly bigger than the Tesla Model S and Lucid Air. It’s closer in size to the long-wheelbase Mercedes-Benz S-Class.

Thanks to BYD’s second-gen Blade Batteries and Flash Charging technology, the vehicle’s battery can charge from 10% to 70% in just 5 minutes. A full recharge (10% to 97%) takes just 9 minutes.

The Great Han joins the Great Tang SUV as the flagship sedan under BYD’s Dynasty lineup. BYD announced that Great Tang sales reached 14,000 units in October, marking four months of sales above 10,000.

There might be hope for Arkane after all: A new report says multiple companies have made offers for it.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A new report from Bloomberg says Dishonored developer Arkane might not be as doomed as it appeared last month, as multiple companies have made offers to acquire the studio.

Citing "people familiar with the negotiations," the Bloomberg report says Ubisoft is among Arkane's suitors, as are at least two other unnamed gaming companies. Xbox has also considered selling the studio to its own management, effectively making it independent, although whether that option is still on the table isn't clear.

Despite producing an enviable slate of acclaimed immersive sims over the years (until it was compelled by senior leadership to do otherwise), Arkane fell victim to the Xbox "reset" in July that saw thousands of employees laid off (with more still awaiting their fates) and four studios cast adrift. Arkane avoided being the fifth, reportedly thanks to France's "Florange Law" that compelled Xbox to search for a buyer before pulling the plug.

The deadline for that search was November 13, after which Arkane would be shuttered—but a few weeks ahead of that date, Microsoft extended it by a month, to "explore options, including finding a partner to support the studio through the development work still ahead," a reference to the Marvel's Blade game Arkane currently has in development. That extension suggested there was at least some hope that Arkane might find a new home, instead of being closed.

Going from Xbox to Ubisoft could be viewed as a "frying pan to fire" move, given Ubisoft's own ongoing financial struggles, layoffs, and closures. But Arkane's immersive sim expertise could be a good fit for some of Ubisoft's biggest properties, including Assassin's Creed, Far Cry, and—yes, I'm just going to say it—Splinter Cell, which would be magnificent in the style of Dishonored. Arkane and Ubisoft both being French companies can't hurt, either.

I've reached out to Xbox and Ubisoft for comment on the report, and will update if I receive a reply.

SEC division views Canton Coin as digital commodity in no-action letter

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

a16z partner Miles Jennings welcomed the SEC staff's position, arguing it could signal a broader regulatory path for blockchain network tokens.

The U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission's Division of Corporation Finance said it views Canton Coin (CC) as a digital commodity and will not recommend enforcement action over its programmatic issuance under certain conditions.

In a no-action letter published Tuesday, the division said Canton Coin derives its value from the programmatic operation of the Canton Network and supply-and-demand dynamics, rather than expectations of profit from the managerial efforts of others. It also cited the network's lack of a central party.

"Based on the facts presented, the Division will not recommend enforcement action to the Commission if, in reliance on your opinion as counsel, Canton Coin is minted programmatically by Super Validators, Validators and application providers, in the manner and under the circumstances described in your letter, without registration under Section 5 of the Securities Act or Section 12(g) of the Exchange Act," Deputy Chief Counsel Jonathan Ingram wrote.

The letter reflects the Division of Corporation Finance's enforcement position based on the specific facts and representations submitted by Canton and does not express a legal conclusion on the issues presented, including whether Canton Coin is a security.

Canton Network is a blockchain infrastructure protocol developed by Digital Asset that aims to enable secure, privacy-enabled, and interoperable transactions across institutions and networks.

In May, 21Shares became the first U.S. ETF issuer to offer direct exposure to CC, the network's native utility token.

In June, Digital Asset raised $355 million in a funding round led by Andreessen Horowitz's a16z crypto fund to expand the Canton ecosystem and accelerate institutional adoption of its blockchain infrastructure.

a16z partner Miles Jennings welcomed the SEC staff's position, arguing it could signal a broader regulatory path for blockchain network tokens.

"Incredible. @CantonNetwork just received no action relief from the SEC for its network token," Jennings wrote Tuesday on X. "The @SECGov's token taxonomy from March and follow up FAQs have now created a clear pathway out of securities laws—Network tokens are digital commodities."

North Korean fake job interview led to $6.3B Binance heist

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

'It was not a single isolated breach or a simple phishing incident,' exchange says

North Korean cryptocurrency thieves tricked a Binance software engineer into downloading malware onto their work-issued computer during a fake job interview process before last week's record-breaking $6.3 billion heist.

In an incident report published on Tuesday, the cryptocurrency exchange said several techniques - including social engineering via fake job interviews, malware downloads, compromised internal AI tooling, endpoint takeover, and an “advanced transaction-authorization method” - led to the October 29 security breach.

“Our current investigation indicates that this was a sophisticated, multi-stage campaign,” the incident report said. “It was not a single isolated breach or a simple phishing incident.”

Over the weekend, the FBI attributed the theft to TraderTraitor, a North Korean hacking operation linked to Lazarus Group, known for combining espionage with massive cryptocurrency heists. The Pyongyang-backed crew is also highly adept at job market scams - both posing as IT workers, who are then hired by Western companies, and as potential employers to lure software engineers into downloading malware and infecting corporate devices.

With the recent Binance attack, the Norks used the latter.

The attack prep began back in June with a supply chain attack when one of its engineers downloaded a malicious Homebrew installation. While the malware was detected by the security team, the device was replaced, and all affected credentials rotated, “Engineer A’s role and his related colleagues on our wallet infrastructure team was exposed to the hacker,” the report says.

Three months later, the social engineering part of the attack happened.

In September, another software engineer was contacted via a “legitimate job-hunting platform” by someone purporting to be a project director who arranged a series of job interviews for the Binance engineer with the fake company. During the first round - a live video interview with the engineer and a fake HR rep - the interviewer “shared a social media account with him, including personal photos and videos posted on the platform, in order to build trust,” according to the report. “This was more advanced and deceptive than previously seen social engineering approaches.”

The second interview with someone purporting to be the CEO of the phony company was conducted on a “popular social chat app,” and toward the end, the CEO instructed the engineer to download an “assignment” containing a malware-laced code repository. The code executed on his work computer during the setup process, which kicked off a series of unauthorized activities.

According to Binance, the malware replaced a legitimate development tool component with a malicious version, which executed commands and established communication with an attacker-controlled external server. The attackers also took steps to hide the malicious script and evade detection, and it worked. Binance’s endpoint security tools did not immediately detect the malware.

CREATE  /opt/homebrew/Cellar/pyenv/2.8.0/libexec/pyenv_

DELETE  .../libexec/pyenv  (3,344 B)      ← remove the valid pyenv

RENAME  pyenv_ → pyenv  (3,414 B)  ← replace it with a malicious version

DELETE  /opt/homebrew/bin/pyenv  (3,414 B)

Then, on October 20, the miscreants used the compromised computer and an internal AI tool to gain access to the computers of wallet DevOps engineer A and another wallet software developer, Engineer C.

Engineer A had a container deployment permission token on his computer, which the Norks soon discovered. On October 29, the attacker accessed Engineer C's computer, used the stolen credentials from Engineer A to deploy a container, and triggered a script to launch the cryptocurrency-stealing attack.

As of Tuesday, Binance said it has rebuilt the compromised wallet system and internal AI tool, and replaced all of the affected devices. It also decommissioned all credentials and tokens assigned to the compromised engineers, and is working with law enforcement, blockchain intel firms, and security researchers to trace and freeze the assets.

The crypto exchange has also hired independent cybersecurity experts to conduct a cybersecurity review of the incident, and recommend additional protective measures. Binance said it’s also working to establish a security research fund and initiatives with AI companies and security shops to focus on AI security in blockchain environments.®

'It was not a single isolated breach or a simple phishing incident,' exchange says

The 2026 midterm elections could change everything for Trump. Here’s what’s at stake

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The midterm elections have the potential to upend everything from politics, to business, to the stock market. But the person most impacted by Tuesday’s results could be President Donald Trump and determine the course of his final two years in office.

On the political front, the battle to control Congress remains front and center with a number of seats in play, including 435 in the U.S. House of Representatives and 35 in the U.S. Senate.

At issue, whether Democrats will gain control of the House of Representatives, or, even, the Senate—effectively breaking the Trump administration and Republicans’ stronghold over Congress.

“The midterms matter in general because they determine whether there is alignment between the executive and the legislative branches,” Harvard Business School professor Vincent Pons said in an interview earlier this year. “Oftentimes, voters vote against the party of the president. Some voters have a preference for divided government, to have checks and balances. If the Republican party was to lose the midterms, either the House or both the House and the Senate, it could have dramatic consequences in terms of circumventing the power of [the administration].”

A Democratic upset could check Trump’s power, paving the way for a lame-duck presidency for the remainder of his term. A blue sweep could also give Dems the votes they need to investigate the president’s finances, his administration’s immigration policies, and bring another impeachment.

In Michigan, Democratic Senate nominee, Abdul El-Sayed, told CNN if elected, “the first thing we’re going to do when [the Democrats] win this Senate seat? Hold Trump accountable. Then, onto the real work–taking on big corporations, taxing billionaires their wealth, and guaranteeing healthcare through Medicare for All.”

In New York’s 12th Congressional district, Democrat Micah Lasher wrote a roadmap for Congressional oversight for if, and when, Democrats regain control of the House, which calls for: “Check[ing] the Trump administration’s lawlessness by making clear that illegal activity and corruption will be investigated, and by spurring criminal prosecutions or impeachments; focus[ing] the American public on the illegal and corrupt activity that gets lost in the daily barrage of news and distraction out of the White House; and shap[ing] the narrative of the 2028 elections and help Democrats win back the White House and grow our majority.”

In California, Democratic Rep. Robert Garcia (D-CA) said he plans to look into White House’s immigration policies, and “demand accountability” for ICE’s tactics. The fallout which could even lead to a Republican upset in 2028.

While, House minority leader, Rep. Hakeem Jeffries (D-NY) said in July “when you put us back in charge of the United States House of Representatives, you’ll get a Congress that speaks up for everyday Americans and a Congress that stands up to an out-of-control president.” Jeffries emphasized to beat Republicans, Democrats were focused on healthcare costs, bringing down the high cost of living, and ending the war in Iran.

A divided Congress, with Republicans maintaining their majority in at least one branch, could result in major political gridlock for the remainder of Trump’s term which could have major repercussions for business.

If Republicans can hold onto their seats, that could empower Trump to continue to accelerate his domestic agenda and policies on key issues like defense and AI—with those government-connected sectors seeing continued growth, and banks and cryptocurrencies retaining lighter regulation.

Binance says hackers used fake job interviews and an internal AI tool to pull off the $6.3 billion heist

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A North Korean hacking unit reportedly started its break-in with a search engine ad that served up a malware download.

A North Korean hacking unit reportedly started its break-in with a search engine ad that served up a malware download.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The largest crypto theft to date was pulled off by combining some well-known hacking techniques over the course of a few months, according to Binance. A postmortem from the company says the process that started with one developer downloading a tampered installer in June apparently spread by a malicious Google ad, exposing details about the engineers and how they work. Then, in September, another engineer was targeted by someone pretending to be interested in hiring them, who set up a series of calls and multiple rounds of interviews, sharing social media pages to build trust, and eventually prompting them to download a fake interview assignment that was actually malware.

Binance claims that its security teams did detect the first installation of malware in June, and replaced the worker’s device, but that its security tools didn’t detect the second attack that deleted the pyenv executable and replaced it with a malicious version that gave them backdoor access:

The malware employed sophisticated obfuscation and evasion techniques, including replacing a legitimate development tool component with a malicious version to execute commands and establish communication with an attacker-controlled external server. The 0-day malware was not detected by the endpoint security protection tools at the time. The attacker also took steps to conceal the malicious activity and remove traces of the compromise, making detection and subsequent investigation more challenging.

By October 20th, the story goes, the attackers, whom the FBI has identified as a North Korean unit dubbed “TraderTraitor,” used Binance’s own internal AI tool to gain control over wallets for the first engineer compromised in June, as well as a third employee, before logging into the third employee’s computer on October 29th and launching their attack.

The postmortem also goes into why Binance wasn’t able to stop the transactions that occurred nearly two hours after the first unauthorized transfers, saying that “Because the hot wallet used Multi-Party Computation (MPC), there was no single private key that could be used to immediately revoke the malicious EIP-7702 authorizations.” Binance says that it’s prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threat cases, and CEO Richard Teng writes that no user is bearing the loss, with about $2.85 billion in stolen assets frozen or intercepted: “At this moment, 98% of impacted tokens had been replenished; we’re completing the remaining 2%. Withdrawals have broadly resumed.”

What’s less clear is why this particular attack worked — the danger of job interviews that ask people to download and execute code on machines with access to employer material has been warned about for years, and there’s little detail on the “internal AI tool” that was used by the attackers and why it was able to help them in the first place.
